Decentralized Online Learning for Random Inverse Problems Over Graphs
Tao Li, Xiwei Zhang, Yan Chen

Abstract
We propose a decentralized online learning algorithm for distributed random inverse problems over network graphs with online measurements, and unify the distributed parameter estimation in Hilbert spaces and the least mean square problem in reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHS-LMS). We transform the convergence of the algorithm into the asymptotic stability of a class of inhomogeneous random difference equations in Hilbert spaces with L 2 L_{2} -bounded martingale difference terms and develop the L 2 L_{2} -asymptotic stability theory in Hilbert spaces. We show that if the network graph is connected and the sequence of forward operators satisfies the infinite-dimensional spatio-temporal persistence of excitation condition, then the estimates of all nodes are mean square and almost surely strongly consistent. Moreover, we propose a decentralized online learning algorithm in RKHS based on non-stationary online data streams, and prove that the algorithm is mean square and almost surely strongly consistent if the operators induced by the random input data satisfy the infinite-dimensional spatio-temporal persistence of excitation condition.



1
Introduction

Inverse problems have wide applications such as medical imaging, geophysics and oil exploration (
[
1
]
). An inverse problem is to determine the system input (cause) from the system output (result).
In reality, measurements are usually affected by external disturbances, and the inverse problems with noisy measurements have been widely studied including the cases with deterministic noises (
[
2
]
) and those with Gaussian white noises (
[
3
]
).
It is of practical significance to consider inverse problems with both randomly time-varying forward operators and random measurement noises. For example, consider the online learning problem in RKHS. Let
\mathscr{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the input space and
(\mathscr{H}_{K},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{K})
be the Hilbert space with Mercer kernel
K:\mathscr{X}\times\mathscr{X}\to\mathbb{R}
. At time instant
k
, the random (with unknown distribution) input data
x(k)\in\mathscr{X}
and the output data
y(k)\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy the measurement equation
y(k)=f_{0}(x(k))+v(k),\ k\geq 0
, where
f_{0}\in\mathscr{H}_{K}
is the unknown function, and
v(k)\in\mathbb{R}
is the random measurement noise. The online learning problem in
\mathscr{H}_{K}
is to reconstruct
f_{0}
based on the online data stream
\{(x(k),y(k))\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
. By the reproducing property of RKHS, the above measurement equation can be written as

y(k)=H(k)f_{0}+v(k),~k\geq 0,

(1)

where
H(k)
is the randomly time-varying forward operator induced by the input data
x(k)
,
satisfying
H(k)f:=\langle f,K(x(k),\cdot)\rangle_{K}
,
\forall\ f\in\mathscr{H}_{K}
.
Thus, the online learning problem in RKHS comes down to an inverse problem associated with the measurement model (
1
). Besides, Dynamic inverse problems in
[
4
]
, including dynamic computerized tomography, dynamic load monitoring and magnetic particle imaging are the inverse problems with time-invariant forward operators.
Most of the existing works on statistical inverse problems assumed the forward operators to be deterministic and time-invariant, which can not cover the measurement model (
1
) (
[
3
]
,
[
5
]
-
[
12
]
).

In addition to online data streams, many practical problems are required to be solved in a decentralized or distributed information structure. The overall large amounts of data are usually divided into several data sets, and the learning process is performed with multiple parallel processors (
[
13
]
).
Decentralized online learning algorithms
for finite-dimensional parameter estimation have been widely studied. Pioneering works on the decentralized online parameter estimation in finite-dimensional spaces were achieved in
[
14
]
-
[
15
]
and fruitful results were obtained in
[
14
]
-
[
21
]
.
Specifically, the decentralized online learning algorithms with randomly spatio-temporal independent observation matrices were proposed in
[
14
]
-
[
16
]
via the collaborative strategy of diffusion. Kar and Moura established a distributed observability condition in
[
15
]
.

Infinite-dimensional supervised online learning in RKHS is another important topic of random inverse problems (
[
22
]
). Based on the systematic study of batch learning in
[
23
]
, rich results of online learning algorithms based on i.i.d. online data streams were obtained in
[
24
]
-
[
28
]
.
Ying and Pontil (
[
24
]
) considered the least-square online gradient descent
algorithm in RKHS and presented a novel capacity independent approach to derive error bounds and convergence results for this algorithm.
Tarr
\grave{\text{e}}
s and Yao (
[
25
]
) showed that the online regularized algorithm can achieve the strong convergence rate of batch learning, and the weak convergence rate is optimal in the sense that it reaches the minimax and individual lower rates.
Dieuleveut and Bach (
[
26
]
) considered the random-design least-squares regression problem within the RKHS framework, and showed that the averaged unregularized LMS algorithm with a given sufficient large step-size can attain optimal rates of convergence for a variety of regimes for the smoothnesses of the optimal prediction function in RKHS.
Shin
et al
. (
[
27
]
) proposed decentralized adaptive learning algorithms over graphs in RKHS in a deterministic framework. Deng
et al
. (
[
28
]
) used the multiplicative operator in the saddle point problem to carve out the communication structure of decentralized networks and proposed a distributed consensus-based online learning algorithm with i.i.d. measurements. The nonlinear online learning problems in RKHS with spatio-temporal independent measurements were studied in
[
29
]
-
[
37
]
.

Up to now,
the existing theory of inverse problems in statistical and stochastic frameworks is far from mature and the existing related results may be divided into three categories: (i) statistical inverse problems based on deterministic time-invariant compact forward operators in Hilbert spaces (
[
3
]
,
[
5
]
-
[
12
]
); (ii) distributed parameter estimation in finite-dimensional spaces (
[
14
]
-
[
21
]
); (iii) decentralized learning based on stationary data, e.g., i.i.d. measurements in RKHS (
[
27
]
-
[
32
]
). Some basic problems are still open, such as

•

inverse problems with randomly time-varying forward operators;

•

to establish a unified framework for random inverse problems in infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces, distributed parameter estimation problems in finite-dimensional spaces and online learning problems in RKHS;

•

to develop a decentralized RKHS learning theory based on non-stationary data streams.

Motivated by the above problems, we consider a class of random inverse problems over graphs, establish a unified framework to deal with the above three types of problems, and propose a decentralized online learning algorithm in Hilbert spaces. The learning algorithm of each node in the network takes the form of consensus plus innovation as in
[
18
]
. The innovation term is to update the node’ estimate by using the node’s own measurement data, and the consensus term is a weighted sum of its own estimate and the estimates of its neighboring nodes. The forward operator of the measurement of each node is randomly time-varying and is not required to satisfy special statistical properties, such as temporal independence (the forward operator of each node over the graph is independent with respect to time), spatial independence (the forward operators of different nodes are independent of each other at each moment) and stationarity, and the random measurement noise is no longer restricted to Gaussian white noise.

We weaken the constraints on the forward operators compared with the most of the existing studies on inverse problems (
[
3
]
-
[
12
]
). We consider the cases with general bounded linear forward operators instead of compact ones. Besides, we allow the forward operators to be randomly time-varying, which are not restricted to be deterministic and time-invariant. These general settings bring essential difficulties to the convergence analysis of the algorithm.
Tarr
\grave{\text{e}}
s and Yao (
[
25
]
), Smale and Yao (
[
37
]
) transformed the online learning problem with i.i.d. data streams in RKHS into the inverse problem with the deterministic time-invariant Hilbert-Schmidt forward operator, and then obtained the convergence result by using the singular value decomposition (SVD) of the linear compact operators in the Hilbert space.
By using the i.i.d. properties of the data, Dieuleveut and Bach (
[
26
]
) transformed the estimation error equation into the random difference equation equivalently, where the homogeneous part is deterministically time-invariant and the inhomogeneous part is the martingale difference sequence in the Hilbert space, from which the mean-square convergence of the algorithm was obtained by means of the spectral decomposition property of the compact operator. Note that the SVD of linear compact operators in Hilbert spaces is only applicable for the inverse problems with deterministic and time-invariant linear compact forward operators. The existing methodologies in
[
6
]
-
[
10
]
,
[
25
]
-
[
26
]
and
[
37
]
are no longer applicable for our problems.

Note that for the decentralized online learning in finite-dimensional spaces over random graphs based on non-stationary data, we have established the stochastic spatio-temporal persistence of excitation (SSTPE) condition to ensure convergence of the algorithm in
[
20
]
. However, for this kind of finite-dimensional excitation conditions, the information matrices are all required to be positive definite, i.e., the eigenvalues of the matrix have strictly positive lower bounds. Obviously, the SSTPE condition is not applicable for inverse problems in infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. It is known that even for a strictly positive compact operator, the infimum of its eigenvalues is zero, and then the SSTPE condition can not hold.

To this end, by means of measurability and integration theory of mappings with values in Banach spaces, spectral decomposition theory of bounded self-adjoint operators, and martingale convergence methods, we investigate the
L_{p}^{q}
-stability condition on the sequence of operator-valued random elements.
We transform the convergence analysis of the algorithm into the
L_{2}
-asymptotic stability of time-varying random difference equations in Hilbert spaces. Since the forward operators of inverse problems in infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces usually do not have bounded inverses, the existing asymptotic stability theory on infinite-dimensional random difference equations with compressive operators in Hilbert spaces cannot be applied to inverse problems (
[
38
]
-
[
42
]
). To this end, we propose the
L_{p}^{q}
-stability condition on the sequence of the products of operator-valued random elements, and establish the
L_{2}
-asymptotic stability theory for a class of inhomogeneous random difference equations in Hilbert spaces with
L_{2}
-bounded martingale difference terms. We give sufficient conditions on the stability of a class of operator-valued random sequences composed of forward operators and Laplacian matrices of graphs. We prove that if the graph is connected, and the sequence of forward operators satisfies the
infinite-dimensional spatio-temporal persistence of excitation
condition, then all nodes’ estimates are all mean square and almost surely strongly consistent with the unknown function of the inverse problem.

We develop a theory of decentralized online learning in RKHS. Almost the existing literature on online supervised learning in RKHS are based on i.i.d. data (e.g.,
[
35
]
-
[
36
]
), while we propose a decentralized online learning algorithm based on non-stationary and non-independent data streams in RKHS. We establish the convergence condition by equivalently transforming the distributed learning problem in RKHS into the random inverse problems over graphs. Especially, if the graph has only one node and the random input data is i.i.d., then our algorithm degenerates to the centralized online learning algorithm without regularization parameters in
[
24
]
and
[
26
]
.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section II, the online random inverse problems over graphs in Hilbert spaces are formulated and the decentralized online learning algorithm is proposed. In Section III, the convergence of the algorithm is proved. In Section IV, the decentralized online learning problems in RKHS are studied. In Sections V and VI, simulation results and conclusions are given, respectively. The proofs of theorems and corollaries in Sections III and IV are in Appendix A. The proofs of all lemmas in Sections III and IV can be found in Appendix C.

The following notations will be used throughout the paper. For a subset
A
of a set
X
, if
x\in A
, then
\mathbf{1}_{A}(x)=1
, and if
x\notin A
, then
\mathbf{1}_{A}(x)=0
. Denote
f\in{\cal F}_{0}
if
f
is measurable with respect to the
\sigma
-algebra
{\cal F}_{0}
. For any given sets
\{A_{i},i\in\mathscr{I}\}
,
where
\mathscr{I}
is a set of indices, denote
\sigma\left(\bigcup_{i\in\mathscr{I}}A_{i}\right)
by
\bigvee_{i\in\mathscr{I}}A_{i}
. Let
\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathscr{X})
be the topology induced by the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{X}}
in a Banach space
\mathscr{X}
. Denote
(\Omega,{\cal F},\mathbb{P})
as a complete probability space.
Let
L^{0}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
be a linear space composed of all mappings which take values in
(\mathscr{X},\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathscr{X}))
and are strongly
\mathbb{P}
-measurable with reference to
(\Omega,{\cal F},\mathbb{P})
. In particular, for a sub-
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{G}
of
{\cal F}
,
L^{0}(\Omega,\mathscr{G};\mathscr{X})
is defined with reference to
(\Omega,\mathscr{G},\mathbb{P}|_{\mathscr{G}})
.
Let
L^{p}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})=\{f\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathscr{X}):\|f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}<\infty\}
, where

\|f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}:=\left(\int_{\Omega}\|f\|_{\mathscr{X}}^{p}\,\text{d}\mathbb{P}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}
,
1\leq p<\infty
.
Let
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{Y},\mathscr{Z})
be the linear space of all bounded linear operators from the Banach space
\mathscr{Y}
to the Banach space
\mathscr{Z}
, in particular,
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{Z}):=\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{Z},\mathscr{Z})
. Let
\tau_{\text{S}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{Y},\mathscr{Z}))
be the strong operator topology of
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{Y},\mathscr{Z})
.

Let
(\mathscr{X}_{i},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathscr{X}_{i}})
be a Hilbert space, where the norm induced by the inner product is defined by
\|x_{i}\|_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}:=\sqrt{\langle x_{i},x_{i}\rangle_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}}
,
x_{i}\in\mathscr{X}_{i}
,
i=1,\cdots,n
. The Hilbert direct sum space is denoted by
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathscr{X}_{i}=\left\{x=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}):x_{i}\in\mathscr{X}_{i},1\leq i\leq n\right\}
,
where the inner product is defined by
\langle x,y\rangle_{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathscr{X}_{i}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle x_{i},y_{i}\rangle_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}
,
\forall\ x=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}),y=(y_{1},\cdots,y_{n})\in\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathscr{X}_{i}
. Denote
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathscr{X}:=\mathscr{X}^{n}
. Let
\mathscr{X}
and
\mathscr{Y}
be Hilbert spaces. Denote the Kronecker product of the vector
\boldsymbol{1}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
f\in\mathscr{X}
by
\boldsymbol{1}_{n}\otimes f:=(f,\cdots,f)\in\mathscr{X}^{n}
and the Kronecker product of the matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
and
B\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})
by

\displaystyle A\otimes B:=\begin{pmatrix}a_{11}B&\cdots&a_{1m}B\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
a_{n1}B&\cdots&a_{nm}B\end{pmatrix}\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}^{m},\mathscr{Y}^{n}).

The operations and properties of operator matrices in Hilbert direct sum spaces can be found in
[
43
]
. For
T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})
,
if an operator
T^{*}:\mathscr{Y}\to\mathscr{X}
satisfies
\langle Tx,y\rangle_{\mathscr{Y}}=\langle x,T^{*}y\rangle_{\mathscr{X}}
,
\forall\ x\in\mathscr{X},y\in\mathscr{Y}
, then
T^{*}
is called the adjoint operator of
T
.
For
T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X})
, if
T^{*}=T
, i.e.,
\langle Tx,y\rangle_{\mathscr{X}}=\langle x,Ty\rangle_{\mathscr{X}}
,
\forall\ x,y\in\mathscr{X}
, then
T
is called a self-adjoint operator. If a self-adjoint operator
T\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X})
satisfies
\langle Tx,x\rangle_{\mathscr{X}}\geq 0
,
\forall\ x\in\mathscr{X}
, then
T
is positive self-adjoint, denoted by
T\geq 0
. Especially if
\langle Tx,x\rangle_{\mathscr{X}}>0
for any given
x\neq 0
in
\mathscr{X}
, then
T
is strictly positive self-adjoint, denoted by
T>0
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=\{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}}\}
denote a weighted graph, where
\mathcal{V}=\{1,...,N\}
is the set of nodes and
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}}
is the set of edges. The unordered pair
(j,i)\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}}
if and only if there exists an edge between nodes
j
and
i
. Denote the set of neighboring nodes of node
i
by
\mathcal{N}_{i}=\{j\in\mathcal{V}:(j,i)\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}}\}
. The matrix
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}}=[a_{ij}]\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
is called the weighted adjacency matrix of
\mathcal{G}
, and for any given
i,j\in\mathcal{V}
,
a_{ii}=0
and
a_{ij}=a_{ji}>0
if and only if
j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}
.
The Laplacian matrix of
\mathcal{G}
is defined by
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}=\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}}-\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}}
, where the degree matrix
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}}=\text{diag}\{\sum_{j=1}^{N}a_{1j},\sum_{j=1}^{N}a_{2j},\cdots,\sum_{j=1}^{N}a_{Nj}\}
.

2
Online Learning for Random Inverse Problems over Graphs

2-A

Online Random Inverse Problems over Graphs

Consider a distributed communication network modeled by a weighted graph
\mathcal{G}=\{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}}\}
consisting of
N
nodes. The measurement
y_{i}(k)
of node
i
at instant
k
is given by

y_{i}(k)=H_{i}(k)f_{0}+v_{i}(k),\ k=0,1,2,...\ i=1,\cdots,N,

(2)

where
f_{0}\in\mathscr{X}
to be estimated is an unknown element in a separable Hilbert space
\mathscr{X}
, the random forward operator
H_{i}(k):\Omega\to\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}_{i})
is an operator-valued random element with values in
(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}_{i}),\tau_{\text{S}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}_{i})))
, and the measurement noise
v_{i}(k):\Omega\to\mathscr{Y}_{i}
is a random element with values in a separable Hilbert space
(\mathscr{Y}_{i},\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathscr{Y}_{i}))
.

Remark 2.1
.

For the finite-dimensional Euclidean space
\mathscr{X}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the measurement model (
2
) has been widely studied in
[
14
]
-
[
21
]
, where the forward operator
H_{i}(k)
degenerates to the random observation matrix and
f_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
degenerates to the unknown finite-dimensional parameter vector. Kar
et al
. (
[
17
]
) investigated the decentralized estimation algorithms with nonlinear measurement models, where they introduced separably estimable measurement models that generalize the observability condition in linear centralized estimation to nonlinear decentralized estimation. It is worth pointing out that even for the nonlinear measurement model in
[
17
]
, the unknown quantity to be estimated is a parameter vector in a finite-dimensional space, different from which, the unknown quantity
f_{0}
in the measurement model (
2
) can be a nonlinear function, which is an element in the infinite-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathscr{X}
. The measurement model (
2
) is essentially different from that in
[
17
]
. This will be further clarified in Remark
4.1
.

Denote
y(k)=(y_{1}(k),\cdots,y_{N}(k)):\Omega\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}
,
v(k)=(v_{1}(k),\cdots,v_{N}(k)):\Omega\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}
and
H(k)=(H_{1}(k),\cdots,H_{N}(k)):\Omega\to\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X},\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i})
.
We can write (
2
) in the compact form

y(k)=H(k)f_{0}+v(k),\ k\geq 0.

(3)

The online random inverse problem means reconstructing
f_{0}
by real-time random measurements
\{y(k),k\geq 0\}
.

The measurement equation (
3
), which covers the existing models of inverse problems, is more general in the sense that the forward operator can be randomly time-varying. Besides, different from the existing literature, which has a centralized information structure, here, the reconstruction of
f_{0}
is constrained by the information structure of the graph, i.e., there is no centralized fusion center collecting the overall measurements
y(k)
, and at each moment
k
, node
i
can only use its own observation
y_{i}(k)
and its neighbors’ estimates
f_{j}(k),j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}
to give its next estimate
f_{i}(k+1):\Omega\to\mathscr{X}
for
f_{0}
, i.e.,

f_{i}(k)\in\left(\bigvee_{s=0,1,2,...,k-1}\sigma(y_{i}(s);\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathscr{Y}_{i}))\right)\bigvee\left(\bigvee_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}\cup\{i\}}\sigma\left(f_{j}(k-1);\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathscr{X})\right)\right),~i\in\mathcal{V}.

Here, the problem of cooperatively estimating
f_{0}
by the nodes over the graph based on each node’s local measurements, its own and neighbors’ estimates is called the
random inverse problem over the graph
.

Remark 2.2
.

Most of the existing literature on inverse problems assumes the forward operator to be a deterministic and time-invariant linear compact operator
H
, associated with the measurement equation

y=Hf_{0}+v.

(4)

Here, different from the existing literature, the forward operator in the measurement equation (
3
) is allowed to be random and time-varying. In the classical inverse problem, the noise
v
in the measurement equation (
4
) is modeled as a deterministic perturbation (
[
44
]
). In the statistical inverse problem, it is modeled as Gaussian white noise (
[
5
]
-
[
6
]
).
Based on the stochastic gradient descent (SGD) algorithm, Lu and Mathé (
[
9
]
), Jahn and Jin (
[
11
]
) and Jin and Lu (
[
12
]
) obtained the centralized learning strategy by the random discrete sampling of the forward operator and minimizing the loss functional

\displaystyle\widetilde{J}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\|y-Hx\|^{2}\right],\ \forall\ x\in\mathscr{X}.

(5)

Specifically, Jahn and Jin (
[
11
]
) and Jin and Lu (
[
12
]
) investigated the regularization property of SGD with a priori and a posteriori stopping rules, and Lu and Mathé (
[
9
]
) gave an upper bound on the estimation error of SGD with discrete level. Recently, Iglesias
et al.
(
[
7
]
) and Lu
et al.
(
[
8
]
) solved the statistical inverse problem based on real-time measurements
\{y(k):y(k)=Hf_{0}+v(k),k\geq 0\}
, where
\{v(k),k\geq 0\}
is an i.i.d. Gaussian white noise sequence. The statistical inverse problems in
[
7
]
-
[
8
]
are the special cases of the online random inverse problem with random forward operators.

2-B

Decentralized Online Learning Algorithm

Denote
\mathcal{H}(k)=\text{diag}\{H_{1}(k),\cdots,H_{N}(k)\}
. Based on the loss functional (
5
), we consider minimizing the loss functional
J:\mathscr{X}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
with the Laplacian regularization term given by

\displaystyle J(f)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbb{E}\Big[\|y(k)-\mathcal{H}(k)f\|_{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}}^{2}\Big]+\left\langle\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\right)f,f\right\rangle_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}\right),~\forall\ f\in\mathscr{X}^{N},

(6)

where
I_{\mathscr{X}}
is the identical operator on the Hilbert space
\mathscr{X}
. The loss functional
J(f)
consists of two terms: the mean-square estimation error term
\mathbb{E}\left[\|y(k)-\mathcal{H}(k)f\|_{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}}^{2}\right]
and the Laplacian regularization term
\langle(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}})f,f\rangle_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}a_{ij}\|f_{i}-f_{j}\|^{2}_{\mathscr{X}}
, where
f_{i}\in\mathscr{X}
is the
i
-th component of
f
.

Suppose
\mathcal{H}(k)\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}^{N},\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}))
and
v(k)\in L^{2}(\Omega;\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i})
.
If the sequences
\{\mathcal{H}(k),k\geq 0\}
and
\{v(k),k\geq 0\}
are both i.i.d, then it follows from Definition
B.3
and Proposition
B.1
.(a) that
\{y(k)-\mathcal{H}(k)f,k\geq 0\}
is a sequence of i.i.d. random elements with values in the Hilbert space
(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i},\tau_{\text{N}}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}))
.
It can be verified that

\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)\in L^{1}(\Omega;\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}^{N}))
. Then, by Proposition
B.3
, the gradient operator
\text{grad}~J:\mathscr{X}^{N}\to\mathscr{X}^{N}
is given by

\displaystyle\text{grad}~J(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\text{grad}~\left(\mathbb{E}\Big[\|y(k)-\mathcal{H}(k)f\|_{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}}^{2}\Big]+\left\langle\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\right)f,f\right\rangle_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\text{grad}~\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)f,f\rangle_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}\right]-\text{grad}~\mathbb{E}\Big[\langle\mathcal{H}(k)f,y(k)\rangle_{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}}\Big]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\text{grad}~\left\langle\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\right)f,f\right\rangle_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\text{grad}~\langle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)\right]f,f\rangle_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-\text{grad}~\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\mathcal{H}(k)f,y(k)\rangle_{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\text{grad}~\left\langle\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\right)f,f\right\rangle_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)]x,y\rangle_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\langle\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)x,y\rangle_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\langle x,\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)y\rangle_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle x,\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)]y\rangle_{\mathscr{X}^{N}},\ \forall\ x,y\in\mathscr{X}^{N}.

Therefore,
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)]:\mathscr{X}^{N}\to\mathscr{X}^{N}
is a self-adjoint operator. Noting that the Laplacian matrix
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}
is positive semi-definite, it follows that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}
is a self-adjoint operator. Then

\text{grad}~\langle\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)]f,f\rangle_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}=2\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)]f,~\forall\ f\in\mathscr{X}^{N},

and

\text{grad}~\langle(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}})f,f\rangle_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}=2(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}})f,~\forall\ f\in\mathscr{X}^{N}.

Noting that
\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)y(k)\in L^{1}(\Omega;\mathscr{X}^{N})
, it follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{1}{t}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\mathcal{H}(k)(f+tg),y(k)\rangle_{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\mathcal{H}(k)f,y(k)\rangle_{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}}\right]\right)

(7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\langle\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)y(k),g\rangle_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}]

(8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)y(k)],g\rangle_{\mathscr{X}^{N}},~\forall\ g\in\mathscr{X}^{N}.

It follows that
\text{grad}~\mathbb{E}[\langle\mathcal{H}(k)f,y(k)\rangle_{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)y(k)]
. Thus, we have

\text{grad}~J(f)=-\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)(y(k)-\mathcal{H}(k)f)]+(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}})f,~\forall\ f\in\mathscr{X}^{N}.

Then we have the stochastic gradient descent (SGD) algorithm in the Hilbert space

\displaystyle f(k+1)=f(k)+a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)(y(k)-\mathcal{H}(k)f(k))-b(k)(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}})f(k),~k\geq 0,

(9)

where
a(k)
and
b(k)
are algorithm gains.
Let
f(k)=(f_{1}(k),\cdots,f_{N}(k))
. From (
9
), we obtain the decentralized online learning algorithm

\displaystyle f_{i}(k+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{i}(k)+a(k)H_{i}^{*}(k)(y_{i}(k)-H_{i}(k)f_{i}(k))

(11)

\displaystyle+b(k)\sum_{j\in{\cal N}_{i}}a_{ij}(f_{j}(k)-f_{i}(k)),~k\geq 0,~i\in\mathcal{V}.

Remark 2.3
.

The algorithm (
11
) takes a form of “consensus+innovations”. This kind of decentralized estimation strategies was first proposed in
[
17
]
-
[
18
]
for estimating the parameters in finite-dimensional spaces. Here,
for the measurement model (
2
), the learning strategy (
11
), which is a stochastic gradient decent algorithm for the Laplacian regularized loss functional (
6
), is exactly the “consensus+innovations” type. The algorithm (
11
) can be regarded as the extention of the “consensus+innovations” type algorithm to the case of non-parametric or infinite-dimensional estimation.

Remark 2.4
.

To implement the algorithm (
11
), the terms
H_{i}^{*}(k)y_{i}(k)
and
H_{i}^{*}(k)H_{i}(k)f_{i}(k)
may be computed instead of computing
H_{i}^{*}(k)
. For some inverse problems, such as the deconvolution problem in
[
47
]
and recovering the unknown initial value in the heat equation with a random diffusivity coefficient in
[
48
]
, these terms can be computed by the numerical integration scheme in
[
49
]
. Besides, these terms can be computed by the cubic spline interpolation method in decentralized online learning problems in RKHS. Generally speaking, the computational complexity of the two terms depends on specific problems and the computational methods employed.

Besides, noting that the computations for each node are all in parallel and there exists a constant
d>0
independent of the total number of nodes
N
such that
\sup_{i=1,\ldots,N,\ N\geq 1}\left|\mathcal{N}_{i}\right|\leq d
, it follows that the computational complexity of the algorithm (
11
) is
O(1),\ N\rightarrow\infty
.

3
Convergence Analysis

Although the algorithm (
9
) is designed by assuming that
\{\mathcal{H}(k)\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}^{N},\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i})),\\
k\geq 0\}
and
\{v(k)\in L^{2}(\Omega;\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}),k\geq 0\}
are both i.i.d, and
\mathcal{H}(k),\ k\geq 0
are random elements with values in
(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}^{N},\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}),\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}^{N},\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i})))
.
In fact, in this section, we will show that even for the non-independence and non-stationarity sequence of operator-valued random elements
\{\mathcal{H}(k),k\geq 0\}
with values in
(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}^{N},\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}),\tau_{\text{S}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}^{N},\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i})))
and the noise sequence
\{v(k),k\geq 0\}
, the algorithm (
11
) still converges under mild conditions.

Denote the global estimation error by
e(k)=f(k)-\boldsymbol{1}_{N}\otimes f_{0}
. Note that
(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}})(\boldsymbol{1}_{N}\otimes f_{0})=0
and
\mathcal{H}(k)(\boldsymbol{1}_{N}\otimes f_{0})=H(k)f_{0}
. Subtracting
\boldsymbol{1}_{N}\otimes f_{0}
on both sides of equation (
9
) yields

\displaystyle e(k+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)-b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}})f(k)+a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)y(k)-\boldsymbol{1}_{N}\otimes f_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)-b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}})(f(k)-\boldsymbol{1}_{N}\otimes f_{0}+\boldsymbol{1}_{N}\otimes f_{0})

\displaystyle+a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)y(k)-\boldsymbol{1}_{N}\otimes f_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)-b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}})e(k)-(a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)

\displaystyle+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}})(\boldsymbol{1}_{N}\otimes f_{0})+a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)y(k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)-b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}})e(k)+a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)(y(k)-\mathcal{H}(k)(\boldsymbol{1}_{N}\otimes f_{0}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)-b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}})e(k)+a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)v(k).

(12)

The estimation error equation (
3
) belongs to the following family of randomly time-varying difference equations

\displaystyle x(k+1)=(I_{\mathscr{X}_{1}}-F(k))x(k)+G(k)u(k),~k\geq 0,

(13)

where
x(k)
is a random element with values in a separable Hilbert space
(\mathscr{X}_{1},\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathscr{X}_{1}))
,
u(k)
is a random element with values in a separable Hilbert space
(\mathscr{X}_{2},\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathscr{X}_{2}))
,
F(k):\Omega\to\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}_{1})
and
G(k):\Omega\to\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}_{2},\mathscr{X}_{1})
are random elements with values in the topological spaces
(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}_{1}),\tau_{\text{S}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}_{1})))
and
(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}_{2},\mathscr{X}_{1}),\tau_{\text{S}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}_{2},\mathscr{X}_{1})))
, respectively. To analyze the convergence of the estimation error equation (
3
), we will first develop an asymptotic stability theory of the randomly time-varying difference equation (
13
).

3-A

Asymptotic Stability of Random Difference Equations in Hilbert Spaces

To rigorously study the asymptotic stability of the random difference equations in the Hilbert space
(\mathscr{X},\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathscr{X}))
, we introduce the following definitions.

Definition 3.1
.

If the sequence of random elements
\{x(k),k\geq 0\}
with values in the Hilbert space
(\mathscr{X},\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathscr{X}))
satisfies
\sup_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\|x(k)\|_{\mathscr{X}}^{p}\right]<\infty
, where
p>0
, then
\{x(k),k\geq 0\}
is said to be
L_{p}
-bounded.

Definition 3.2
.

If the sequence of random elements
\{x(k),k\geq 0\}
with values in the Hilbert space
(\mathscr{X},\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathscr{X}))
satisfies
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\|x(k)\|_{\mathscr{X}}^{p}\right]=0
, where
p>0
, then
\{x(k),k\geq 0\}
is said to be
L_{p}
-asymptotically stable.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\{A(k),k\geq 0\}
be a sequence of operator-valued random elements with values in
(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}),\tau_{\text{S}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X})))
and
\{\mathcal{F}(k),k\geq 0\}
be a filter in
(\Omega,{\cal F},\mathbb{P})
. If for any given
L_{q}
-bounded adaptive sequence
\{x(k),{\cal F}(k),k\geq 0\}
with values in the Hilbert space
\mathscr{X}
,

\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\prod_{k=n+1}^{m}A(k)x(n)\right\|_{\mathscr{X}}^{p}\right]=0,\ \forall\ n\geq 0,\ \text{where}\ p,\ q>0,

then
\{A(k),k\geq 0\}
is said to be
L_{p}^{q}
-stable with respect to the filter
\{\mathcal{F}(k),k\geq 0\}
.

The strong convergence of the sequence of products of deterministic non-expansive operators has attracted the attentions of many scholars. By assuming strong convergence of operator products, the convergence results on infinite-dimensional deterministic time-varying difference equations in a general metric space were obtained (
[
45
]
-
[
46
]
). Reich and Zaslavski (
[
45
]
) studied deterministic time-varying compressive operators in general metric spaces and obtained strong convergence results on the sequence of operator products; Pustylnik
et al.
(
[
46
]
) studied the strong convergence of the sequence of operator products consisting of finite number of projection operators.

Noting that a sequence of deterministic operator products converging strongly to
0
can be regarded as a
L_{p}^{q}
-stable sequence of operators w.r.t. the trivial filter
\{{\cal F}(k)=\{\emptyset,\Omega\},k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
, the concept of strong convergence for the sequence of operator products in the above literature can be regarded as a special case of Definition
3.3
. Besides, for the case in finite-dimensional spaces, Guo (
[
50
]
) proposed the concept of
L_{p}
-exponentially stable random matrix sequence
\{I-B(k)\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N},k\geq 0\}
, i.e. there exist constants
M>0
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\prod_{k=n+1}^{m}(I-B(k))\right\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}^{p}\right]\leq M\lambda^{m-n},\ \forall\ m>n\geq 0.

From Definition
3.3
, we know that

\{I-B(k)\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N},k\geq 0\}
is
L^{s}_{r}
-stable w.r.t. any given filter
\{\mathcal{F}(k),k\geq 0\}
, where
r=pa^{-1}
,
s=pba^{-1}
and
a,b
are positive real numbers with
a^{-1}+b^{-1}=1
.

Denote

{\cal F}(k)=\bigvee_{i=0}^{k}(\sigma(F(i);\tau_{\text{S}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}_{1})))\bigvee\sigma(G(i);\tau_{\text{S}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}_{2},\mathscr{X}_{1})))\bigvee\sigma(u(i);\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathscr{X}_{2})))
,
k\\
\geq 0
and
{\cal F}(-1)=\{\emptyset,\Omega\}
.

For the
L_{2}
-asymptotic stability of the solution sequence of the random difference equation (
13
), we have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

For the random difference equation (
13
), let
\{u(k),{\cal F}(k),k\geq 0\}
be a
L_{2}
-bounded sequence of martingale differences, and the sequence
\{u(k),k\geq 0\}
be independent of
\{F(k),k\geq 0\}
and
\{G(k),k\geq 0\}
. If (i)
\{I_{\mathscr{X}_{1}}-F(k),k\geq 0\}
is
L_{2}^{2}
-stable w.r.t.
\{{\cal F}(k),k\geq 0\}
, (ii) there exists a sequence of nonnegative real numbers
\{\gamma(k),k\geq 0\}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|I_{\mathscr{X}_{1}}-F(k)\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}_{1})}^{4}\Big|\mathcal{F}(k-1)\right]\leq 1+\gamma(k)~\text{a.s.},

(14)

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\gamma(k)<\infty
, and (iii)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\|G(k)\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}_{2},\mathscr{X}_{1})}^{2}\right]<\infty,

(15)

\displaystyle\sup_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\|G(k)\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}_{2},\mathscr{X}_{1})}^{4}\right]<\infty,

(16)

then, the solution
\{x(k),k\geq 0\}
of (
13
) is
L_{2}
-asymptotically stable.

Remark 3.1
.

Systematic results on the stability of randomly time-varying difference equations in finite-dimensional spaces were achieved in
[
50
]
-
[
53
]
, while the results on randomly time-varying difference equations in infinite-dimensional spaces remain fragmented. Kubrusly (
[
39
]
), Vajjha
et al.
(
[
40
]
) and the references therein transformed the analysis of the mean square convergence of stochastic approximation algorithms in Hilbert spaces into the
L_{2}
-asymptotic stability analysis of randomly time-varying difference equation. Ungureanu
et al.
(
[
38
]
), Ungureanu (
[
41
]
) and Zhang
et al.
(
[
42
]
) investigated the
L_{2}
-asymptotic stability of the solution sequence of the randomly time-varying difference equation
x(k+1)=A(k)x(k)+b(k)
in Hilbert space. The above literature assumed
A(k):\Omega\to\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X})
to be mean square exponentially stable, i.e., there exist constants
M>0
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
such that, for any
m>n\geq 0,~\forall\ x\in\mathscr{X}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\left(\prod_{k=n+1}^{m}A(k)\right)x\right\|_{\mathscr{X}}^{2}\right]\leq M\lambda^{m-n}\|x\|^{2}.

(17)

For the randomly time-varying difference equation (
13
), even if
F(k)\equiv F
, where
F
is a deterministic self-adjoint compact operator with
\|F\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X})}\leq 1
, the operator
A(k)\equiv I_{\mathscr{X}}-F
does not satisfy (
17
). In fact, if (
17
) holds, then

\displaystyle\left\|\prod_{k=n+1}^{n+2^{l}}A(k)\right\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X})}\leq\sqrt{M}\lambda^{2^{l-1}},\ \forall\ l,n\geq 0.

(18)

Noting that
\|I_{\mathscr{X}}-F\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X})}=\sup\limits_{\|x\|_{\mathscr{X}}=1}|\langle(I_{\mathscr{X}}-F)x,x\rangle_{\mathscr{X}}|=1-\inf_{\|x\|_{\mathscr{X}}=1}\langle Fx,x\rangle_{\mathscr{X}}=1
, thus we have
\mathbb{E}\Big[\big\|\prod_{k=n+1}^{n+2^{l}}A(k)\big\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X})}^{2}\Big]=\big\|(I_{\mathscr{X}}-F)^{2^{l}}\big\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X})}=\big\|I_{\mathscr{X}}-F\big\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X})}^{2^{l}}=1,\ \forall\ l,\ n\geq 0,

which is in contradiction to (
18
). Thereby, the existing results and methods on the stability of infinite-dimensional random difference equations are not applicable to random inverse problems.

3-B

Convergence of the Decentralized Algorithm

Let

\displaystyle{\cal F}(k)=\bigvee_{i=0}^{k}\left(\sigma\left(\mathcal{H}(i);\tau_{\text{S}}\left(\mathscr{L}\left(\mathscr{X}^{N},\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{j}\right)\right)\right)\bigvee\sigma\left(v(i);\tau_{\text{N}}\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{j}\right)\right)\right),~k\geq 0,

(19)

and
{\cal F}(-1)=\{\emptyset,\Omega\}
, where
\mathcal{H}(i)
and
v(i)
are given in Section 2.2. We need the following assumptions.

Assumption 3.1
.

The noises
\{v(k),k\geq 0\}
with values in
(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i},\tau_{\text{N}}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}))
and the random forward operator sequence
\{H(k),k\geq 0\}
with values in
(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X},\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}

\mathscr{Y}_{i}),\tau_{\text{S}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}
,
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i})))
are mutually independent.

Assumption 3.2
.

The noises
\{v(k),{\cal F}(k),k\geq 0\}
are martingale differences and there exists a constant
\beta_{v}>0
such that
\sup_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{E}\big[\|v(k)\|_{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}}^{2}|{\cal F}(k-1)\big]\leq\beta_{v}~\text{a.s.}

For the gains of the algorithm (
11
), we need the following conditions.

Condition 3.1
.

The algorithm gains
\{a(k),k\geq 0\}
and
\{b(k),k\geq 0\}
are both monotonically decreasing sequences of positive real numbers.

Condition 3.2
.

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{2}(k)<\infty
and
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}b^{2}(k)<\infty
.

Condition 3.3
.

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a(k)=\infty
and
\max\{a(k)-a(k+1),b(k)-a(k)\}=\mathcal{O}(a^{2}(k)+b^{2}(k))
.

We analyze the convergence of the algorithm (
11
) now.
Firstly, from Lemma
3.1
, we have the following key theorem.

Theorem 3.1
.

For the algorithm (
11
), suppose that Assumptions
3.1
,
3.2
and Condition
3.2
hold, there exists a sequence of nonnegative real numbers
\{\gamma(k),k\geq 0\}
with
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\gamma(k)<\infty
, such that

\displaystyle~~~\left.\mathbb{E}\left[\|I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-\left(a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\right)\|^{4}_{\mathscr{L}\left(\mathscr{X}^{N}\right)}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]

(20)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\gamma(k)~\text{a.s.},

(21)

and the sequence of operator-valued random elements
\{I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-(a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}),k\geq 0\}
is
L_{2}^{2}
-stable w.r.t.
\{{\cal F}(k),k\geq 0\}
.

I. If

\sup_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathcal{H}(k)\|_{\mathscr{L}\left(\mathscr{X}^{N},\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}\right)}^{2}\right]<\infty,

then the algorithm (
11
) is mean square consistent, i.e.,
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\|f_{i}(k)-f_{0}\|_{\mathscr{X}}^{2}]=0,~i\in\mathcal{V}
.

II. If there exists a constant
\rho_{0}>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|\mathcal{H}(k)\|_{\mathscr{L}\left(\mathscr{X}^{N},\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}\right)}^{2}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]\leq\rho_{0}~\text{a.s.},

then the algorithm (
11
) is almost surely strongly consistent, i.e.,
\lim_{k\to\infty}\|f(k)-f_{0}\|_{\mathscr{X}}=0~\text{a.s.},~i\in\mathcal{V}
.

We will next give intuitive sufficient conditions on the mean square and almost sure strong consistency of the algorithm (
11
). At first, we need the following fundamental lemma.

Lemma 3.2
.

For the algorithm (
11
), let
\mathcal{G}
be connected and assume that Assumptions
3.1
,
3.2
and Conditions
3.1
-
3.3
hold. If there exist positive self-adjoint operators
\mathscr{H}_{i}\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X})
,
i=1,\cdots,N
satisfying
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{H}_{i}>0
, and a fixed-length time period
h>0
, such that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathscr{H}_{j}x(k)-\sum_{i=kh}^{(k+1)h-1}\mathbb{E}\left.\left[H_{j}^{*}(i)H_{j}(i)x(k)\right|{\cal F}(kh-1)\right]\right\|_{\mathscr{X}}^{2}\right]<\infty,

(22)

for any
L_{2}
-bounded adaptive sequence
\{x(k),{\cal F}(kh-1),k\geq 0\}
with values in the Hilbert space
\mathscr{X}
, and there exists a constant
\rho_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{k\geq 0}\left(\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)\|_{\mathscr{L}\left(\mathscr{X}^{N}\right)}^{2^{\max\{h,2\}}}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2^{\max\{h,2\}}}}\leq\rho_{0}~\text{a.s.},

(23)

then
\{I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)-b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}},k\geq 0\}
is
L_{2}^{2}
-stable w.r.t.
\{{\cal F}(k),k\geq 0\}
.

Combining Theorem
3.1
and Lemma
3.2
, we have the following theorem.

Theorem 3.2
.

For the algorithm (
11
),
suppose that all the conditions in Lemma
3.2
hold. If there exists a sequence of nonnegative real numbers
\{\Gamma(k),k\geq 0\}
with
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Gamma(k)<\infty
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-4\left(a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\right)\|_{\mathscr{L}\left(\mathscr{X}^{N}\right)}\big|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]\leq 1+\Gamma(k)~\text{a.s.},

(24)

then the algorithm (
11
) is both mean square and almost surely strongly consistent.

Especially, if
\{\mathcal{H}(k),k\geq 0\}

and
\{v(k),k\geq 0\}

are both i.i.d. and they are mutually independent, then the following corollary follows from Theorem
3.2
.

Corollary 3.1
.

For the algorithm (
11
), assume that
\mathcal{G}
is connected, Assumptions
3.1
,
3.2
and Conditions
3.1
-
3.3
hold, and
\{\mathcal{H}(k),k\geq 0\}
and
\{v(k),k\geq 0\}
are both i.i.d. sequences and they are mutually independent. If there exists a constant
\rho_{0}>0
such that
\|\mathcal{H}(0)\|\leq\rho_{0}~\text{a.s.}
and

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\|H_{j}(0)x\|_{\mathscr{Y}_{j}}^{2}\right]>0,\ \forall\ x\in\mathscr{X}\setminus\{0\},

(25)

then the algorithm (
11
) is both mean square and almost surely strongly consistent.

The graph
\mathcal{G}
describes the communication topology among nodes, and its connectivity ensures the nodes to collaboratively reconstruct the unknown function
f_{0}
successfully. The condition (
22
) in Lemma
3.2
plays an important role in the convergence analysis of the decentralized algorithm, which we call the
infinite-dimensional spatio-temporal persistence of excitation
condition.
Note that

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{i=kh}^{(k+1)h-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\|H_{j}(i)x\|_{\mathscr{Y}_{j}}^{2}\right]-\left\langle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathscr{H}_{j}x,x\right\rangle_{\mathscr{X}}\right|

(26)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigg|\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Bigg\langle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\sum_{i=kh}^{(k+1)h-1}\mathbb{E}\left.\left[H^{*}_{j}(i)H_{j}(i)x\right|{\cal F}(kh-1)\right]-\mathscr{H}_{j}x\Bigg],x\Bigg\rangle_{\mathscr{X}}\Bigg|

(27)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Bigg[\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\Bigg\|\sum_{i=kh}^{(k+1)h-1}\mathbb{E}\left.\left[H_{j}^{*}(i)H_{j}(i)x\right|{\cal F}(kh-1)\right]-\mathscr{H}_{j}x\Bigg\|_{\mathscr{X}}^{2}\Bigg]\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}\|x\|_{\mathscr{X}},~x\in\mathscr{X},~k\geq 0.

Therefore, the infinite-dimensional spatio-temporal persistence of excitation condition is equivalent to the combination of the following two conditions.

•

For any
x\in\mathscr{X}\setminus\{0\}
, there exists an integer
K(x)>0
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{i=kh}^{(k+1)h-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|H_{j}(i)x\right\|_{\mathscr{Y}_{j}}^{2}\right]>0,~k\geq K(x);

(28)

•

there exist deterministic time-invariant operators
\mathscr{H}_{j}
,
j=1,\cdots,N
such that

\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\bigg\|\mathscr{H}_{j}x(k)\\
-\sum\limits_{i=kh}^{(k+1)h-1}\mathbb{E}\left[H_{j}^{*}(i)H_{j}(i)x(k)|{\cal F}(kh-1)\right]\bigg\|_{\mathscr{X}}^{2}\Bigg]<\infty,

for any
L_{2}
-bounded adaptive sequence
\{x(k),{\cal F}(kh-1),k\geq 0\}
with values in the Hilbert space
\mathscr{X}
.

The
spatio-temporal persistence of excitation
implies that
the non-zero orbits of the random forward operators of all nodes are non-degenerate in the mean square sense for a fixed length time period, where
spatio-temporal
refers specifically to the temporal and spatial states of the operator orbit
x\mapsto H_{j}(i)x
of the random forward operator. By (
28
), we know that we neither need the temporal orbit of the forward operator of each node over the graph to be non-degenerate, i.e.,
\sum_{i=kh}^{(k+1)h-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\|H_{j}(i)x\|_{\mathscr{Y}_{j}}^{2}\right]>0,\ \forall\ j\in\mathcal{V},
nor need the spatial orbit of the forward operator of all nodes to be non-degenerate at each instant, i.e.,
\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\|H_{j}(i)x\|_{\mathscr{Y}_{j}}^{2}\right]>0,\ \forall\ i\geq 0.

Notably, if the sequences of random forward operator and noises are both i.i.d. and they are mutually independent, then the
infinite-dimensional spatio-temporal persistence of excitation
condition degenerates to the case that the spatial orbits of the forward operators of all nodes are non-degenerate at the initial moment
k=0
, i.e., the condition (
25
) in the Corollary
3.1
.

In the past decades, to solve the problems of finite-dimensional parameter estimation and signal tracking with non-stationary and non-independent data, many scholars have proposed excitation conditions based on the conditional expectation of the observation/regression matrix. The stochastic persistence of excitation condition was first proposed by Guo (
[
51
]
) in the analysis of centralized Kalman filtering algorithms. Xie and Guo (
[
54
]
) proposed the cooperative information condition based on the conditional expectations of the observation matrices for the distributed adaptive filtering algorithm over connected graphs. Wang
et al.
(
[
20
]
) proposed the stochastic spatio-temporal persistence of excitation condition for the decentralized online estimation algorithm over randomly time-varying graphs.
Zhang
et al.
(
[
55
]
) proved that if the graph is connected and the randomly time-varying regression matrix satisfies the uniformly conditionally spatio-temporally joint observability condition, i.e., there exists an integer
h>0
and a constant
\theta>0
, respectively, such that

\displaystyle\inf_{k\geq 0}\lambda_{\text{min}}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{i=kh}^{(k+1)h-1}\mathbb{E}\left[H_{j}^{T}(i)H_{j}(i)\big|{\cal F}(kh-1)\right]\bigg)\geq\theta\ \text{a.s.},

then the algorithm achieves mean square and almost sure convergence. For this case, it is not difficult to verify that the random matrix sequence
\{I_{Nn}-a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)-b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{n},k\geq 0\}
satisfies the
L_{2}^{2}
-stability condition w.r.t.
\{{\cal F}(k),k\geq 0\}
, and thus the excitation conditions proposed in
[
20
]
and
[
55
]
are all special cases of the
L_{2}^{2}
-stability condition in Theorem
3.1
.

The persistence of excitation conditions proposed for finite-dimensional systems all require that the conditional expectation of the information matrix consisting of the observation (regression) matrices is positive definite, i.e., the information matrix has strictly positive minimum eigenvalues; however, inverse problems in infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces are usually ill-posed. Even for a strictly positive linear compact operator, the excitation condition similar to the persistence excitation conditions for finite-dimensional systems can not hold any more since the infimum of the eigenvalues of the compact operator is always zero.

4
Decentralized Online Learning in Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space

We will discuss in this section a special class of online random inverse problems: decentralized online learning problems in RKHS. Let
\mathscr{X}
be a non-empty subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
K:\mathscr{X}\times\mathscr{X}\to\mathbb{R}
be a Mercer kernel, and
(\mathscr{H}_{K},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{K})
be a separable RKHS with kernel
K
, which consists of functions with domain
\mathscr{X}
.
The observation data
y_{i}(k)
of the
i
-th node at instant
k
is given by

\displaystyle y_{i}(k)=f_{0}(x_{i}(k))+v_{i}(k),\ k\geq 0,\ i\in\mathcal{V},

(29)

where
x_{i}(k):\Omega\to\mathscr{X}
is a random vector with values in the Hilbert space
(\mathscr{X},\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathscr{X}))
at instant
k
, called the random input data, and the observation noise
v_{i}(k):\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is a random vector with values in the Hilbert space
(\mathbb{R},\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathbb{R}))
, and
f_{0}:\mathscr{X}\to\mathbb{R}
is an unknown function in
\mathscr{H}_{K}
. The nodes cooperatively estimate
f_{0}
by information exchanging among them.

Remark 4.1
.

In
[
17
]
, the measurement of each node is given by
y_{i}(k)=f_{i}(\theta^{*})+v_{i}(k)
,
i=1,2,...,N
, in which the nonlinear mappings
f_{i}(\cdot)
,
i=1,2,...,N
are completely known in prior and it is the parameter vector
\theta^{*}
in a finite-dimensional space, which is unknown and needs to be estimated. While for the measurement model (
29
), it is the mapping
f_{0}
in the infinite-dimensional space
\mathscr{H}_{K}
, which is unknown and needs to be estimated.

For any given
x\in\mathscr{X}
, the function
K_{x}:\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{H}_{K}
induced by the Mercer kernel is given by
K_{x}(y)=K(x,y),\ \forall\ y\in\mathscr{X}.

Define the random forward operator
H_{i}(k)
as

H_{i}(k)(f):=f(x_{i}(k)),~f\in\mathscr{H}_{K},~k\geq 0,~i\in\mathcal{V},

then the measurement model (
29
) can be represented as the random inverse problem with the measurement equation (
2
). Based on the algorithm (
11
), the decentralized online learning strategy in
\mathscr{H}_{K}
is given by

\displaystyle\hskip-22.76228ptf_{i}(k+1)=f_{i}(k)+a(k)(y_{i}(k)-f_{i}(k)(x_{i}(k)))K_{x_{i}(k)}+b(k)\sum_{j\in{\cal N}_{i}}a_{ij}(f_{j}(k)-f_{i}(k)),

(30)

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~k\geq 0,~i\in\mathcal{V}.

(31)

The learning process of (
30
) is given by Algorithm
1
.
Assume that
\mathscr{X}
is a bounded and closed interval of
\mathbb{R}
in Algorithm

.

L
points
\{z_{l},\ l=1,\ldots,L\}
at equal distances on
\mathscr{X}
.

, weighted matrix
A=(a_{ij})
, algorithms gains
\{a(k),b(k),\ k\geq 0\}
, kernel function
K(\cdot,\cdot)
.

for

k=1
, …,
K_{1}

do

4:

for

, …,
N

do

Input:
x_{i}(k)
,
y_{i}(k)
.

if

x_{i}(k)\in\{z_{l},\ l=1,\ldots,L\}

then

for

l=1
, …,

f_{i}(k+1)(z_{l})=f_{i}(k)(z_{l})+a(k)(y_{i}(k)-f_{i}(k)(x_{i}(k)))K(x_{i}(k),z_{l})

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +b(k)\sum_{j\in{\cal N}_{i}}a_{ij}(f_{j}(k)(z_{l})-f_{i}(k)(z_{l})).

Approximate

.

for

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +b(k)\sum_{j\in{\cal N}_{i}}a_{ij}(f_{j}(k)(z_{l})-f_{i}(k)(z_{l})).

and

, if

, then output

, and if
x\notin\{z_{l},\ l=1,\ldots,L\}
, then output
f_{i}(K_{1})(x)
by the cubic spline interpolation method based on
\{f_{i}(K_{1})(z_{l}),l=1,\ldots,L\}
.

Algorithm 1

Decentralized Online Learning in RKHS

Given
\phi,\psi\in\mathscr{H}_{K}
, denote the rank 1 tensor product operator
\phi\otimes\psi:\mathscr{H}_{K}\to\mathscr{H}_{K}
by

\left(\phi\otimes\psi\right)(f):=\langle f,\psi\rangle_{K}\phi,~f\in\mathscr{H}_{K}.

For the algorithm (
30
), we need the following assumptions.

Assumption 4.1
.

\sup_{x\in\mathscr{X}}K(x,x)<\infty
.

Assumption 4.2
.

The sequence
\{x_{i}(k),i\in\mathcal{V},k\geq 0\}
of random vectors with values in the Hilbert space
(\mathscr{X},\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathscr{X}))
and the sequence
\{v_{i}(k),i\in\mathcal{V},k\geq 0\}
of random variables with values in the Hilbert space
(\mathbb{R},\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathbb{R}))
are mutually independent.

Lemma 4.1
.

If Assumption
4.1
and Assumption
4.2
hold, then
H_{i}(k)
is a random element with values in
(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}_{K},\mathbb{R}),\tau_{\text{S}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}_{K},\mathbb{R}))),\ k\geq 0,~i\in\mathcal{V}
.

By Lemma
4.1
and (
19
), it is known that

\displaystyle{\cal F}(k)=\bigvee_{s=0}^{k}\bigg(\sigma\left(\mathcal{H}(s);\tau_{\text{S}}\left(\mathscr{L}\left(\mathscr{H}_{K}^{N},\mathbb{R}^{N}\right)\right)\right)\bigvee\sigma\left(v(s);\tau_{\text{N}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{N}\right)\right)\bigg),~k\geq 0,

(32)

and
{\cal F}(-1)=\{\emptyset,\Omega\}
, where
v(s)=(v_{1}(s),\cdots,v_{N}(s))
and
\mathcal{H}(s)=\text{diag}\{H_{1}(s),\cdots,H_{N}(s)\},\ s\geq 0
.

Assumption 4.3
.

The noises
\{v_{i}(k),{\cal F}(k),k\geq 0\}
,
i\in\mathcal{V}
are martingale difference sequences and there exists a constant
\beta>0
, such that
\max\limits_{i\in\mathcal{V}}\sup\limits_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{E}\big[\|v_{i}(k)\|_{\mathbb{R}}^{2}|{\cal F}(k-1)\big]\leq\beta~\text{a.s.}

Remark 4.2
.

The existing works on RKHS online learning (
[
24
]
-
[
37
]
) all require the random input data to be i.i.d. Notice that in Assumption
4.2
, the sequence of random forward operators with values in
(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X},\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}),\tau_{\text{S}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X},\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i})))
is not required to satisfy special statistical properties such as independence, stationarity, etc.

Based on the convergence results of the algorithm (
11
), we can obtain the following convergence results.

Theorem 4.1
.

For the algorithm (
30
), suppose that
\mathcal{G}
is connected, Assumptions
4.1
-
4.3
and Conditions
3.1
-
3.3
hold. If there exist positive self-adjoint operators
N_{i}\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}_{K})
,
i=1,\cdots,N
satisfying
\sum_{i=1}^{N}N_{i}>0
and there exists a fixed-length time period
h>0
, such that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\Bigg\|\Bigg(N_{j}-\sum_{i=kh}^{(k+1)h-1}\mathbb{E}\left.\left[K_{x_{j}(i)}\otimes K_{x_{j}(i)}\right|{\cal F}(kh-1)\right]\Bigg)g(k)\Bigg\|_{K}^{2}\right]<\infty,

(33)

for arbitrary
L_{2}
-bounded adaptive sequence
\{g(k),{\cal F}(kh-1),k\geq 0\}
, then the algorithm (
30
) is both mean square and almost surely strongly consistent.

We now give some corollaries.

Corollary 4.1
.

For the algorithm (
30
), suppose that
\mathcal{G}
is connected, Assumptions
4.1
-
4.3
and Conditions
3.1
-
3.3
hold. If there exist positive self-adjoint operators
N_{i}\in\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}_{K})
,
i=1,\cdots,N
satisfying
\sum_{i=1}^{N}N_{i}>0
, and there exists a fixed-length time period
h>0
, a constant
\mu_{0}>0
and a nonnegative real sequence
\{\tau(k),k\geq 0\}
, such that

\displaystyle\max_{j\in\mathcal{V}}\Bigg\|N_{j}-\sum_{i=kh}^{(k+1)h-1}\mathbb{E}\left.\left[K_{x_{j}(i)}\otimes K_{x_{j}(i)}\right|{\cal F}(kh-1)\right]\Bigg\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}_{K})}^{2}\leq\mu_{0}\tau(k)~\text{a.s.},

(34)

where
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\tau(k)<\infty
, then the algorithm (
30
) is both mean square and almost surely strongly consistent. Besides, the algorithm (
30
) is pointwisely almost surely strongly consistent, that is,
\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}f_{i}(k)(x)=f_{0}(x)

a.s.
,
\forall\ x\in\mathscr{X},\ i\in\mathcal{V}
.

Remark 4.3
.

Noting that Assumption
4.1
implies that
K_{x_{j}(i)}\otimes K_{x_{j}(i)}
is a Bochner integrable random element with values in the Banach space
(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}_{K}),\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{H}_{K})))
, then the conditional expectations
\mathbb{E}[K_{x_{j}(i)}\otimes K_{x_{j}(i)}|{\cal F}(kh-1)]
,
i,k\geq 0
,
j\in\mathcal{V}
uniquely exist by Lemma
B.3
.

Especially, if the input data
\{(x_{1}(k),\cdots,x_{N}(k)),k\geq 0\}
are i.i.d, then we have the following corollary.

Corollary 4.2
.

For the algorithm (
30
), suppose that
\{(x_{1}(k),\cdots,x_{N}(k)),k\geq 0\}
and
\{(v_{1}(k),\cdots,\\
v_{N}(k)),k\geq 0\}
are i.i.d. sequences and they are mutually independent, and
\mathcal{G}
is connected. If Assumptions
4.1
-
4.3
and Conditions
3.1
-
3.3
hold, and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{N}K_{x_{j}(0)}\otimes K_{x_{j}(0)}\Bigg]>0,

(35)

then the algorithm (
30
) is both mean square and almost surely strongly consistent.

Remark 4.4
.

For the centralized online learning with input data drawn independently from the probability measure
\rho_{\mathscr{X}}
on
\mathscr{X}
, Tarrès and Yao (
[
25
]
) defined the covariance operator of the probability measure
\rho_{\mathscr{X}}
in
\mathscr{H}_{K}
as
L_{K}:\mathscr{H}_{K}\to\mathscr{H}_{K}
,

L_{K}(f)(y)=\int_{\Omega}K(x,y)f(x)\,\text{d}\rho_{\mathscr{X}},~\forall\ f\in\mathscr{H}_{K}.

By the reproducing property and Assumption
4.1
, it follows that
L_{K}=\mathbb{E}[K_{x}\otimes K_{x}]
. This implies that for the centralized online learning problem in RKHS with i.i.d. data, the condition (
35
) in Corollary
4.2
just degenerates to that in
[
25
]
: the covariance operator
L_{K}>0
.

5
Numerical Simulation

We consider an undirected connected graph with a node set
\mathcal{V}=\{1,2,\ldots,10\}
and its weighted adjacency matrix is given by
A=[a_{i,j}]
, where
a_{1,2}=a_{2,1}=0.2,

a_{1,4}=a_{4,1}=0.4
,
a_{2,3}=a_{3,2}=0.1
,
a_{2,4}=a_{4,2}=0.3
,
a_{3,5}=a_{5,3}=0.5
,
a_{4,5}=a_{5,4}=0.6
,
a_{4,6}=a_{6,4}=0.8
,
a_{5,6}=a_{5,6}=0.7,

a_{6,7}=a_{7,6}=0.3
,
a_{7,8}=a_{8,7}=0.2
,
a_{8,9}=a_{9,8}=0.9
,
a_{9,10}=a_{10,9}=0.1
and for the remaining positions,
a_{i,j}=0.

For
i\in\mathcal{V}
, the observation data of node
i
at instant
k
is
(x_{i}(k),y_{i}(k))
, where

y_{i}(k)=f^{*}(x_{i}(k))+v_{i}(k)
,
f^{*}(x)=e^{-(x-1)^{2}},\ \forall\ x\in\mathscr{X}=[-2,4]
is the unknown true function to be estimated, the input data
x_{i}(k),\ i=1,2,\ldots,10,\ k\in\mathbb{N}
are independent random variables sampling according to the following rules. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{i}(2k)
and
x_{i}(2k+1)
are with uniform distributions on
\big[-2,4-\frac{3}{k+1}\big]
and
\big[\frac{3}{k+1}-2,4\big]
, respectively. Take the kernel function as
K(x,y)=e^{-(x-y)^{2}},\ \forall\ x,\ y\in\left[-2,4\right]
and we know that the unknown true function
f^{*}=K(x,1)\in\mathscr{H}_{K}
, where
\mathscr{H}_{K}
is the RKHS with kernel
K
.

We sample
1000
points
\{z_{l},\ l=1,\ldots,1000\}
with
z_{l}=-2+\frac{6(l-1)}{1000},\ l=1,\ldots,1000.
We use the algorithm (
30
) to iterate the values of
f_{i}(k)
at the sampled points, that is,

\displaystyle f_{i}(k+1)(z_{l})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{i}(k)(z_{l})+a(k)(y_{i}(k)-f_{i}(k)(x_{i}(k)))K(x_{i}(k),z_{l})+b(k)\sum_{j\in{\cal N}_{i}}a_{ij}(f_{j}(k)(z_{l})-f_{i}(k)(z_{l})),

(36)

where
f_{i}(0)(z_{l})=0
,
\ l=1,\ldots,1000,\ k\geq 0,~i\in\mathcal{V}
.
If
x_{i}(k)\notin\{z_{l},\ l=1,\ldots,1000\}
, then we get the approximation of
f_{i}(k)(x_{i}(k))
by the cubic spline interpolation method.

The measurement noises
v_{i}(k),\ i\in\mathcal{V},\ k\in\mathbb{N}
are the i.i.d. Gaussian noises with the normal distribution
N(0,0.1)
and are independent of the input data. The algorithm gains are

a(k)=(k+1)^{-0.6},\ b(k)=(k+1)^{-1},\forall\ k\geq 1.

Fig. 1(a) and Fig. 1(b) show the values of the true function and the outputs
f_{i}(k),\ i\in\mathcal{V}
of (
36
), i.e.
the values of
f^{*}
and
f_{i}(k),\ i\in\mathcal{V}
at all sampled points in
[-2,4]
for
k=1000
and
k=100000
iterations, respectively. It can be seen that the estimations of all nodes
f_{i}(k),\ i\in\mathcal{V}
converge to the unknown true function
f^{*}
pointwisely almost surely as
k
increases. This is consistent with Corollary
4.1
.

In addition to Gaussian measurement noises, Laplace measurement noises also appear in some signal processing problems (
[
56
]
). We assume that
v_{i}(k),\ k\in\mathbb{N},\ i\in\mathcal{V}
are the i.i.d. Laplace noises with mean
0
and variance
2
and are independent of the input data. The algorithm gains are

a(k)=(k+1)^{-0.8},\ b(k)=(k+1)^{-0.9},\forall\ k\geq 1.
Fig.
2
(a) and Fig.
2
(b) show the values of the true function and the outputs
f_{i}(k),\ i\in\mathcal{V}
of (
36
).
It can be seen that the estimations of all nodes
f_{i}(k),\ i\in\mathcal{V}
converge to the unknown true function
f^{*}
pointwisely almost surely as
k
increases. This is consistent with Corollary
4.1
.

Fig. 1:
Gaussian measurement noises: (a) estimates
f_{i}(k),\ i=1,\cdots,10
of nodes for
k=1000
; (b) estimates
f_{i}(k),\ i=1,\cdots,10
of nodes for
k=100000
.

Fig. 2:
Laplace measurement noises: (a) estimates
f_{i}(k),\ i=1,\cdots,10
of nodes for
k=1000
; (b) estimates
f_{i}(k),\ i=1,\cdots,10
of nodes for
k=100000
.

6
Conclusions

We have established a framework of random inverse problems with online measurements over graphs, and present a decentralized online learning algorithm with online data streams.
It is not required that the random forward operators satisfy special statistical assumptions such as mutual independence, spatio-temporal independence or stationarity. Each node updates its estimate at the next instant by using its new observation and a weighted sum of its own and neighbors’ estimates.
Firstly, by exploiting the probabilistic properties of random elements with values in different topological spaces in a stochastic framework, we propose the
L_{p}^{q}
-stability condition on the sequence of operator-valued random elements, and establish the
L_{2}
-asymptotic stability theory of a class of inhomogeneous random difference equations in Hilbert spaces with
L_{2}
-bounded martingale difference terms. Subsequently, we transform the asymptotic stability of these kinds of infinite-dimensional random difference equations into the
L_{2}^{2}
-stability condition on the operator-valued random elements. We have obtained an intuitive sufficient condition on the convergence of decentralized online learning algorithms for random inverse problems over graphs which is imposed on the random forward operators and the Laplacian matrix of the graph, i.e., the
infinite-dimensional spatio-temporal persistence of excitation
condition. We have proved that if the forward operators over connected graphs satisfy the
infinite-dimensional spatio-temporal persistence of excitation
condition, then all nodes’ estimates are mean square and almost surely strongly consistent. Finally, by equivalently transforming the distributed learning problem in RKHS to the random inverse problem over graphs, we propose a decentralized online learning algorithm in RKHS with non-stationary online data streams, and prove that the algorithm is mean square and almost surely strongly consistent if the operators induced by the random input data satisfy the
infinite-dimensional spatio-temporal persistence of excitation
condition.

There are still many promising directions deserving for future research. (i) The case with multiplicative measurement noises. Only additive measurement noises are considered in (
2
),
while multiplicative noises appear in some realistic scenarios, such as synthetic aperture radar images (
[
57
]
). For this case, the estimation error equation belongs to a class of randomly time-varying difference equations that do not satisfy the independence condition required in Lemma
3.1
.
(ii) The case with unbalanced and directed communication graphs. For this case, the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}
may not be positive, which implies that Lemma
3.2
does not hold. It is crucial to develop novel proof techniques or leverage the algorithm presented in
[
58
]
for addressing unbalanced directed graphs.
(iii) Extension to the distributed fixed-point finding problem (
[
59
]
) with measurement noises. Li
et al
. (
[
59
]
) investigated the distributed fixed-point finding problem for a global operator. Two algorithms are proposed for two scenarios, both of which use constant algorithm gains and are proven to achieve linear convergence. Noting that the decaying algorithm gains in (
11
) are introduced to attenuate the measurement noises, thus the convergence rate of (
11
) can not be linear. While
[
59
]
does not consider the case with the measurement noises of the global operator, it is worth extending our method to address the problem in
[
59
]
with measurement noises.

Appendix A
Proofs of Theorems and Corollaries in Sections 3-4

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Denote
F(k)=a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}
and
G(k)=a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)
, respectively. Notice that
F(k)\geq 0
and the estimation error equation (
3
) can be rewritten as the following random difference equation

e(k+1)=\left(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-F(k)\right)e(k)+G(k)v(k).

We will briefly sketch the proof. Firstly, choosing algorithm gains properly,
we verify the conditions in Lemma
3.1
and establish conclusion I. Then, by the nonnegative
supermartingale convergence theorem, we demonstrate that
\|e(k)\|^{2}
converges almost surely. This together with conclusion I leads to conclusion II.

Given the initial value
e(0)\in\mathscr{X}^{N}
, by Proposition
B.1
.(a)-(c), we know that
\{e(k),k\geq 0\}
is a random sequence with values in the Hilbert space
(\mathscr{X}^{N},\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathscr{X}^{N}))
. On the one hand, it follows from Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
that
\{v(k),k\geq 0\}
is independent of
\{F(k),G(k),k\geq 0\}
, and
\sup_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{E}[\|v(k)\|^{2}]\leq\beta_{v}
. On the other hand, by the condition (
20
), we get
\mathbb{E}[\|I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-F(k)\|^{4}|{\cal F}(k-1)]\leq 1+\gamma(k)~\text{a.s.}
, which gives

\displaystyle~~~~\mathbb{E}\left[\|a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\|^{2}\right]

(A.1)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\|I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-F(k))\|^{2}\right]

(A.2)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(1+\mathbb{E}\left[\|I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-F(k)\|^{2}\right]\right)

(A.3)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(1+\mathbb{E}\left[\|I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-F(k)\|^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

(A.4)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(1+\sqrt{1+\gamma(k)}\right).

(A.5)

Noting that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}
is positive semi-definite, we have
a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\geq a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\\
\mathcal{H}(k)\geq 0~\text{a.s.}
, which leads to
\|a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)\|^{2}\leq\|a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\|^{2}
. By (
A.1
), we obtain

\displaystyle~~~~\sup_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\|G(k)\|^{4}\right]

(A.6)

\displaystyle=\sup_{k\geq 0}\left\{a^{2}(k)\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)\right\|^{2}\right]\right\}

(A.7)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{k\geq 0}\left\{a^{2}(k)\mathbb{E}\left[\|a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\|^{2}\right]\right\}

(A.8)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{k\geq 0}\left\{a^{2}(k)\left(1+\sqrt{1+\gamma(k)}\right)\right\}

(A.9)

\displaystyle<\infty,

(A.10)

where the last inequality is obtained from Condition
3.2
and
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\gamma(k)<\infty
.

(I) If
\sup_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathcal{H}(k)\|^{2}]<\infty
, it follows from Condition
3.2
that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\|G(k)\|^{2}\right]\leq\left\{\sup_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathcal{H}(k)\|^{2}\right]\right\}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{2}(k)<\infty.

By Lemma
3.1
, the sequence of solutions to the estimation error equation (
3
) is
L_{2}
-asymptotically stable, i.e.,
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\|f_{i}(k)-f_{0}\|^{2}]=0,~i\in\mathcal{V}
.

(II) If
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathcal{H}(k)\|^{2}|{\cal F}(k-1)]\leq\rho_{0}~\text{a.s.}
, on the one hand, noting that
\sup_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathcal{H}(k)\|^{2}]\leq\rho_{0}<\infty
, by the above conclusion I, we have
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\|e(k)\|^{2}]=0
. On the other hand, it follows from the condition (
20
) and (
A.6
) that

\displaystyle\sup_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\|(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-F(k))G(k)\|^{2}\right]\leq\sup_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\|I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-F(k)\|^{4}+\|G(k)\|^{4}\right]<\infty.

Thus, by (
3
), Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
, Proposition 2.6.31 in
[
60
]
and Proposition
B.6
, we get

\displaystyle~~~~\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|e(k+1)\|^{2}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|\left(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-F(k)\right)e(k)+G(k)v(k)\|^{2}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-F(k))e(k)\|^{2}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]+\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|G(k)v(k)\|^{2}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]

\displaystyle~~~+2\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\langle e(k),(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-F(k))G(k)v(k)\rangle\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-F(k))e(k)\|^{2}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]+\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|G(k)v(k)\|^{2}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]

\displaystyle~~~+2\langle e(k),\mathbb{E}\left.\left[(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-F(k))G(k)v(k)\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-F(k))e(k)\|^{2}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]+\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|G(k)v(k)\|^{2}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]

\displaystyle~~~+2\langle e(k),\mathbb{E}\left.\left[(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-F(k))G(k)\mathbb{E}[v(k)|{\cal F}(k-1)]\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-F(k))e(k)\|^{2}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]+\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|G(k)v(k)\|^{2}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-F(k))\|^{2}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]\|e(k)\|^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|G(k)v(k)\|^{2}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-F(k))\|^{4}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\|e(k)\|^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|G(k)v(k)\|^{2}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\gamma(k)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|e(k)\|^{2}+\beta_{v}\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|G(k)\|^{2}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\gamma(k)\right)\|e(k)\|^{2}+\rho_{0}\beta_{v}a^{2}(k)~\text{a.s.}

Noting that
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\gamma(k)<\infty
and
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{2}(k)<\infty
, it follows from Lemma
D.1
that
\|e(k)\|^{2}
converges almost surely, which together with
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\|e(k)\|^{2}]=0
gives
\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}e(k)=0~\text{a.s.}

\hfill\square

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

By the conditions (
22
)-(
23
) and Lemma
3.2
, it is known that
\{I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)-b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}},k\geq 0\}
is
L_{2}^{2}
-stable w.r.t.
\{{\cal F}(k),k\geq 0\}
. Denote
D(k)=a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}
. By the condition (
23
), we know that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|D(k)\|^{r}|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[\|a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}})\|^{2^{h}}\big|{\cal F}(k-1)\big]\Big)^{\frac{r}{2^{h}}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\max\{a(k),b(k)\}^{r}\Big(2^{2^{h}-1}\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)\|^{2^{h}}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]+2^{2^{h}-1}\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\|^{2^{h}}\Big)^{\frac{r}{2^{h}}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{r}\left(a^{r}(k)+b^{r}(k)\right)\bigg(\left(\mathbb{E}\left.\left[\|\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)\|^{2^{h}}\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]\right)^{\frac{r}{2^{h}}}+\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\|^{r}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{r}(a^{r}(k)+b^{r}(k))(\rho_{0}^{r}+\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\|^{r})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{r}(a^{r}(k)+b^{r}(k))\rho_{1}^{r}~\text{a.s.},~\forall\ 1\leq r\leq 4,

(A.11)

where
\rho_{1}=\rho_{0}+\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\|
. By the condition (
24
) and (
A
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-\left(a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\right)\|^{4}|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\big\|(I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-(a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}))^{4}\big\||{\cal F}(k-1)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\big\|I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-4D(k)+6D^{2}(k)-4D^{3}(k)+D^{4}(k)\big\||{\cal F}(k-1)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\|I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-4D(k)\|+6\|D(k)\|^{2}+4\|D(k)\|^{3}+\|D(k)\|^{4}|{\cal F}(k-1)\Big]\leq 1+\gamma(k)~\text{a.s.},

where
\gamma(k)=\Gamma(k)+6(a^{2}(k)+b^{2}(k))\rho_{1}^{2}+4(a^{3}(k)+b^{3}(k))\rho_{1}^{3}+(a^{4}(k)+b^{4}(k))\rho_{1}^{4}
. From Condition
3.2
and the condition (
24
), we know that
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\gamma(k)<\infty
, which together with Theorem
3.1
implies that algorithm (
11
) is both mean square and almost surely strongly consistent.

\hfill\qed

Proof of Corollary
3.1
.

We will briefly sketch the proof. By the independence theory of random elements with values in topological spaces and the measurability theory of mappings with values in topological spaces, we
construct
\mathscr{H}_{j},\ j\in\mathcal{V}
satisfying the conditions in Theorem
3.2
and verify the other conditions in Theorem
3.2
.

Noting that
\{\mathcal{H}(k),k\geq 0\}
is an i.i.d. sequence with values in the space
(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}^{N},\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i}),\\
\tau_{\text{S}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}^{N},\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{Y}_{i})))
, from Definition
B.7
, it can be verified that
\{\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)x,\ x

\in\mathscr{X}^{N},k\geq 0\}
is an i.i.d. sequence with values in the Banach space
(\mathscr{X}^{N},\tau_{\text{N}}(\mathscr{X}^{N}))
. By Proposition E.1.10 in
[
61
]
, we know that
\{\|\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)\|,k\geq 0\}
and
\{\|I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-(a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}})\|,k\geq 0\}
are both independent random sequences. By Condition
3.2
, there exists an integer
s_{0}>0
, such that
a(k)+b(k)\leq(4\rho^{2}_{0}+4\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\|)^{-1}
,
\forall\ k\geq s_{0}
. We define the nonnegative real sequence
\{\Gamma(k),k\geq 0\}
as

\displaystyle\Gamma(k)=\begin{cases}4(a(k)+b(k))\left(\rho^{2}_{0}+\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\|\right),&0\leq k<s_{0};\\
0,&k\geq s_{0},\end{cases}

which shows that
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Gamma(k)<\infty
. Noting that
\{\mathcal{H}(k),k\geq 0\}
are i.i.d. and

P\{\|\mathcal{H}(0)\|\leq\rho_{0}\}=1
, it can be verified that
P\{\|\mathcal{H}(k)\|\leq\rho_{0}\}=1
,
k=0,1,...
, and

\\
\|I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-4(a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}})\|\leq 1+4(a(k)+b(k))(\rho^{2}_{0}+\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\|)~\text{a.s.}
,
\forall\ k\geq 0
. Then, we obtain

\displaystyle~~~\left.\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-4\left(a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\right)\right\|\right|{\cal F}(k-1)\right]

(A.12)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-4\left(a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\right)\right\|\right]

(A.13)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sup_{\|x\|=1}\left|\left\langle I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-4\left(a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\right)x,x\right\rangle\right|\bigg]

(A.14)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sup_{\|x\|=1}\left|1-4\left\langle\left(a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\right)x,x\right\rangle\right|\bigg]

(A.15)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg[1-4\inf_{\|x\|=1}\left\langle\left(a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}}\right)x,x\right\rangle\bigg]

(A.16)

\displaystyle\leq 1~\text{a.s.},~\forall\ k\geq s_{0}.

Then, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\|I_{\mathscr{X}^{N}}-4(a(k)\mathcal{H}^{*}(k)\mathcal{H}(k)+b(k)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\otimes I_{\mathscr{X}})\||{\cal F}(k-1)\big]

\displaystyle\leq
